But after a few minutes I’ll realize my eyes have been moving over the page or I’ve been sitting there listening, but I didn’t really take in what was said.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulty sitting still, acting on impulse, or having trouble focusing is causing problems for you in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s starting to cause problems at work. It’s come up in my reviews now—my supervisors have pointed out unfinished tickets and mistakes that slow the team down. I am trying hard, and I usually do fine with customers, but those things are still showing up.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble sitting still or paying attention, even when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, looking back, I think it’s been there a long time. Even in grade school, teachers would write things like I was bright but careless, or that I talked out of turn. I remember getting reminded a lot to stay on my work. So it’s not just something that started with this job.\n\nDoctor: Do you often fidget or feel restless when you're sitting or standing still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Even if I’m trying to sit there and look calm, my foot will be bouncing under the desk, or I’ll be clicking a pen in a meeting without realizing I’m doing it. It’s not usually a big thing, but it happens a lot.\n\nDoctor: Did you have difficulty with organization and time management as a kid?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t remember a lot of specific problems with organizing things or managing time as a kid. I know teachers had to remind me sometimes, but as far as being disorganized or always late, I can’t really say for sure.\n\nDoctor: As a kid, did you often run, climb, or play more than other kids when you were supposed to be playing quietly?\n\nPatient: I don’t remember a lot about running or climbing more than other kids, honestly. What I do notice, even now, is quiet activities don’t last long for me. Like if I’m reading at home or watching a movie with my wife, I’ll start shifting around, making comments, or getting up for something before too long.\n\nDoctor: Are your symptoms causing problems in your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure it’s causing major problems there. I mean, I don’t think my family or friends would say it’s ruining anything. Maybe it annoys people sometimes, but I haven’t noticed it being a big relationship issue.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your wife or anyone else in your personal life commenting on your behavior or habits?\n\nPatient: Yeah, mostly my wife notices it. Like if we’re watching a movie, I’ll start shifting around or making little comments, or I’ll get up to do something instead of just sitting through it. Same thing with reading at home — it’s supposed to be relaxing, but I don’t usually stay with it very long.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

(Note: In adolescents or adults, may be limited to feeling restless.)\n  manifestation: Sitting still through a training video or a slow appointment makes me feel kind of boxed in, like I need to stand up, stretch, or do something with my hands.\n- key: S013\n  name: Quiet_Activity_Difficulty\n  description: Often unable to play or engage in leisure activities quietly.\n  manifestation: Quiet stuff that’s supposed to relax me, like reading at home or sitting through a movie with my wife, doesn’t last long before I’m shifting around, commenting, or getting up for something.\n- key: S014\n  name: Constant_Activity\n  description: Is often 'on the go', acting as if 'driven by a motor'.\n  manifestation: What brings me in is that I feel like I’m always moving from one thing to the next, like I can’t just idle, and the new work setup has made that a lot harder to hide.\n- key: S017\n  name: Waiting_Turn_Difficulty\n  description: Often has difficulty waiting his or her turn.\n  manifestation: Waiting for my turn in a team huddle or in the help queue makes me antsy, and I catch myself checking the screen over and over or wanting to jump in before I’m called on.\n- key: S018\n  name: Interrupting_Intruding\n  description: Often interrupts or intrudes on others (e.g., butts into conversations, games, or activities).\n  manifestation: I don’t mean to be rude, but if I hear coworkers working through a customer problem, I’ll jump in with an answer before they’ve finished or before anyone asked me.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: Looking back, this was around long before this job change; I mean, I don’t think my family or friends would say it’s ruining anything. Maybe it annoys people sometimes, but I haven’t noticed it being a big relationship issue.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your wife or anyone else in your personal life commenting on your behavior or habits?\n\nPatient: Yeah, mostly my wife notices it. Like if we’re watching a movie, I’ll start shifting around or making little comments, or I’ll get up to do something instead of just sitting through it. Same thing with reading at home — it’s supposed to be relaxing, but I don’t usually stay with it very long.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any of these symptoms, like fidgeting or acting on impulse, when you were very young, like in preschool?\n\nPatient: Preschool I’m not really sure — I don’t remember that far back very well. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I feel like I’m constantly going from one thing to the next. Like I can’t really just sit still or idle, even when I should be able to. And with the new setup at work, it’s been harder to cover that up or work around it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sometimes. At work especially, if I hear a coworker working through a customer issue, I’ll just jump in with the answer before they’re done talking or before anyone’s asked me. I don’t mean to be rude, it just comes out.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to focus on one task for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Like in longer coaching meetings, or if I’m reading a new procedure at work, I’ll start off okay. But after a few minutes I’ll realize my eyes have been moving over the page or I’ve been sitting there listening, but I didn’t really take in what was said.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulty sitting still, acting on impulse, or having trouble focusing is causing problems for you in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s starting to cause problems at work. It’s come up in my reviews now—my supervisors have pointed out unfinished tickets and mistakes that slow the team down. I am trying hard, and I usually do fine with customers, but those things are still showing up.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble sitting still or paying attention, even when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, looking back, I think it’s been there a long time. Even in grade school, teachers would write things like I was bright but careless, or that I talked out of turn. I remember getting reminded a lot to stay on my work. It’s not usually a big thing, but it happens a lot.\n\nDoctor: Did you have difficulty with organization and time management as a kid?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t remember a lot of specific problems with organizing things or managing time as a kid. I know teachers had to remind me sometimes, but as far as being disorganized or always late, I can’t really say for sure.\n\nDoctor: As a kid, did you often run, climb, or play more than other kids when you were supposed to be playing quietly?\n\nPatient: I don’t remember a lot about running or climbing more than other kids, honestly. What I do notice, even now, is quiet activities don’t last long for me. Like if I’m reading at home or watching a movie with my wife, I’ll start shifting around, making comments, or getting up for something before too long.\n\nDoctor: Are your symptoms causing problems in your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure it’s causing major problems there. I mean, I don’t think my family or friends would say it’s ruining anything. Maybe it annoys people sometimes, but I haven’t noticed it being a big relationship issue.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your wife or anyone else in your personal life commenting on your behavior or habits?\n\nPatient: Yeah, mostly my wife notices it. Like if we’re watching a movie, I’ll start shifting around or making little comments, or I’ll get up to do something instead of just sitting through it. Same thing with reading at home — it’s supposed to be relaxing, but I don’t usually stay with it very long.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any of these symptoms, like fidgeting or acting on impulse, when you were very young, like in preschool?\n\nPatient: Preschool I’m not really sure — I don’t remember that far back very well. But I do know it was around when I was in grade school. Teachers would write things like I was bright but careless, or that I talked out of turn and needed reminders to stay with my work. So it wasn’t just something that started recently.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or any other family members mention that you had trouble sitting still or paying attention when you were little?\n\nPatient: I don’t remember my parents saying a lot specifically, but looking back, it was definitely around when I was in grade school. Teachers would write that I was bright but careless, that I talked out of turn, and that I needed reminders to stay with my work. So it was there long before this job change.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I feel like I’m constantly going from one thing to the next. At work especially, if I hear a coworker working through a customer issue, I’ll just jump in with the answer before they’re done talking or before anyone’s asked me. I don’t mean to be rude, it just comes out.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to focus on one task for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Like in longer coaching meetings, or if I’m reading a new procedure at work, I’ll start off okay. But after a few minutes I’ll realize my eyes have been moving over the page or I’ve been sitting there listening, but I didn’t really take in what was said.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulty sitting still, acting on impulse, or having trouble focusing is causing problems for you in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s starting to cause problems at work. It’s come up in my reviews now—my supervisors have pointed out unfinished tickets and mistakes that slow the team down. I am trying hard, and I usually do fine with customers, but those things are still showing up.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble sitting still or paying attention, even when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, looking back, I think it’s been there a long time. Even in grade school, teachers would write things like I was bright but careless, or that I talked out of turn. I remember getting reminded a lot to stay on my work. So it’s not just something that started with this job.\n\nDoctor: Do you often fidget or feel restless when you're sitting or standing still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Even if I’m trying to sit there and look calm, my foot will be bouncing under the desk, or I’ll be clicking a pen in a meeting without realizing I’m doing it. It’s not usually a big thing, but it happens a lot.\n\nDoctor: Did you have difficulty with organization and time management as a kid?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t remember a lot of specific problems with organizing things or managing time as a kid. I know teachers had to remind me sometimes, but as far as being disorganized or always late, I can’t really say for sure.\n\nDoctor: As a kid, did you often run, climb, or play more than other kids when you were supposed to be playing quietly?\n\nPatient: I don’t remember a lot about running or climbing more than other kids, honestly. What I do notice, even now, is quiet activities don’t last long for me. Like if I’m reading at home or watching a movie with my wife, I’ll start shifting around, making comments, or getting up for something before too long.\n\nDoctor: Are your symptoms causing problems in your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure it’s causing major problems there. So it wasn’t just something that started recently.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or any other family members mention that you had trouble sitting still or paying attention when you were little?\n\nPatient: I don’t remember my parents saying a lot specifically, but looking back, it was definitely around when I was in grade school. Teachers would write that I was bright but careless, that I talked out of turn, and that I needed reminders to stay with my work. I can tell it annoys people sometimes, like I’m butting in.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms at home, at work, or in other settings outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s definitely not just at work. At home, even quiet things that are supposed to be relaxing can be hard for me. Like if I’m reading, I don’t stay with it long before I’m shifting around. Or sitting through a movie with my wife, I’ll start making comments or get up to do something. It’s not terrible, but it happens enough that I notice it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever lost a friend or had a friendship end because of your behavior or habits?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve lost a friend over it. I mean, I’m sure I’ve irritated people here and there, but I can’t point to a friendship ending because of how I act or my habits.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I know teachers had to remind me sometimes, but as far as being disorganized or always late, I can’t really say for sure.\n\nDoctor: As a kid, did you often run, climb, or play more than other kids when you were supposed to be playing quietly?\n\nPatient: I don’t remember a lot about running or climbing more than other kids, honestly. What I do notice, even now, is quiet activities don’t last long for me. Like if I’m reading at home or watching a movie with my wife, I’ll start shifting around, making comments, or getting up for something before too long.\n\nDoctor: Are your symptoms causing problems in your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure it’s causing major problems there. I mean, I don’t think my family or friends would say it’s ruining anything. Maybe it annoys people sometimes, but I haven’t noticed it being a big relationship issue.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your wife or anyone else in your personal life commenting on your behavior or habits?\n\nPatient: Yeah, mostly my wife notices it. Like if we’re watching a movie, I’ll start shifting around or making little comments, or I’ll get up to do something instead of just sitting through it. Same thing with reading at home — it’s supposed to be relaxing, but I don’t usually stay with it very long.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any of these symptoms, like fidgeting or acting on impulse, when you were very young, like in preschool?\n\nPatient: Preschool I’m not really sure — I don’t remember that far back very well. But I do know it was around when I was in grade school. Teachers would write things like I was bright but careless, or that I talked out of turn and needed reminders to stay with my work. So it wasn’t just something that started recently.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or any other family members mention that you had trouble sitting still or paying attention when you were little?\n\nPatient: I don’t remember my parents saying a lot specifically, but looking back, it was definitely around when I was in grade school. Teachers would write that I was bright but careless, that I talked out of turn, and that I needed reminders to stay with my work. So it was there long before this job change.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or paying attention when you're doing things you enjoy or find interesting?\n\nPatient: Yeah, even then sometimes. If it’s something I’m interested in, I can start off okay, but if it goes on for a while—like a longer coaching meeting or reading through a new procedure—I’ll realize my eyes are still moving over the words, but I didn’t really absorb it. Same with someone talking for a few minutes; I’ll come back and realize I missed part of what was said.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your symptoms affecting how you interact with friends or causing them to react differently to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so, more at work than with friends. I don’t mean to be rude, but if I hear coworkers talking through a customer problem, I’ll sometimes jump in with the answer before they’ve finished, or before anyone asked me. I can tell it annoys people sometimes, like I’m butting in.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms at home, at work, or in other settings outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s definitely not just at work. At home, even quiet things that are supposed to be relaxing can be hard for me. Like if I’m reading, I don’t stay with it long before I’m shifting around. Or sitting through a movie with my wife, I’ll start making comments or get up to do something. It’s not terrible, but it happens enough that I notice it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever lost a friend or had a friendship end because of your behavior or habits?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve lost a friend over it. I mean, I’m sure I’ve irritated people here and there, but I can’t point to a friendship ending because of how I act or my habits.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your behavior or habits have strained your relationship with your wife?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a little. Nothing major, but with my wife it can be annoying for her. Like if we’re trying to watch a movie, I’ll start shifting around, making comments, or I’ll get up to do something instead of just sitting there. Same with reading at home — I don’t stay settled very long. I think it can wear on her sometimes.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. So it wasn’t just something that started recently.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or any other family members mention that you had trouble sitting still or paying attention when you were little?\n\nPatient: I don’t remember my parents saying a lot specifically, but looking back, it was definitely around when I was in grade school. Teachers would write that I was bright but careless, that I talked out of turn, and that I needed reminders to stay with my work. So it was there long before this job change.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or paying attention when you're doing things you enjoy or find interesting?\n\nPatient: Yeah, even then sometimes. If it’s something I’m interested in, I can start off okay, but if it goes on for a while—like a longer coaching meeting or reading through a new procedure—I’ll realize my eyes are still moving over the words, but I didn’t really absorb it. Same with someone talking for a few minutes; I’ll come back and realize I missed part of what was said.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your symptoms affecting how you interact with friends or causing them to react differently to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so, more at work than with friends. I don’t mean to be rude, but if I hear coworkers talking through a customer problem, I’ll sometimes jump in with the answer before they’ve finished, or before anyone asked me. I can tell it annoys people sometimes, like I’m butting in.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms at home, at work, or in other settings outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s definitely not just at work. At home, even quiet things that are supposed to be relaxing can be hard for me. Like if I’m reading, I don’t stay with it long before I’m shifting around. Or sitting through a movie with my wife, I’ll start making comments or get up to do something. It’s not terrible, but it happens enough that I notice it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever lost a friend or had a friendship end because of your behavior or habits?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve lost a friend over it. I mean, I’m sure I’ve irritated people here and there, but I can’t point to a friendship ending because of how I act or my habits.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your behavior or habits have strained your relationship with your wife?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a little. Nothing major, but with my wife it can be annoying for her. Like if we’re trying to watch a movie, I’ll start shifting around, making comments, or I’ll get up to do something instead of just sitting there. Same with reading at home — I don’t stay settled very long. I think it can wear on her sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms consistently across different settings, such as at home, at work, and in social situations, for most of your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, looking back, it’s been there a long time, not just since this job or anything. Even in grade school, teachers would say I was bright but careless, or that I talked out of turn and needed reminders to stay on my work. So I guess it has shown up in different parts of my life for a long time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Same with someone talking for a few minutes; I’ll come back and realize I missed part of what was said.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your symptoms affecting how you interact with friends or causing them to react differently to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so, more at work than with friends. I don’t mean to be rude, but if I hear coworkers talking through a customer problem, I’ll sometimes jump in with the answer before they’ve finished, or before anyone asked me. I can tell it annoys people sometimes, like I’m butting in.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms at home, at work, or in other settings outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s definitely not just at work. At home, even quiet things that are supposed to be relaxing can be hard for me. Like if I’m reading, I don’t stay with it long before I’m shifting around. Or sitting through a movie with my wife, I’ll start making comments or get up to do something. It’s not terrible, but it happens enough that I notice it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever lost a friend or had a friendship end because of your behavior or habits?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve lost a friend over it. I mean, I’m sure I’ve irritated people here and there, but I can’t point to a friendship ending because of how I act or my habits.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your behavior or habits have strained your relationship with your wife?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a little. Nothing major, but with my wife it can be annoying for her. Like if we’re trying to watch a movie, I’ll start shifting around, making comments, or I’ll get up to do something instead of just sitting there. Same with reading at home — I don’t stay settled very long. I think it can wear on her sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms consistently across different settings, such as at home, at work, and in social situations, for most of your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, looking back, it’s been there a long time, not just since this job or anything. Even in grade school, teachers would say I was bright but careless, or that I talked out of turn and needed reminders to stay on my work. So I guess it has shown up in different parts of my life for a long time.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your symptoms have significantly strained or damaged a close relationship?\n\nPatient: Significantly damaged, no, I wouldn’t say that. But it has strained things a bit with my wife. Like quiet things that are supposed to be relaxing — reading at home or watching a movie together — I don’t last very long before I’m shifting around, making comments, or getting up to do something. I can tell that gets annoying for her sometimes.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.
